## Page 1


OBSTRUCTIONS TO SMOOTHING ORBICURVES AND ORBIFOLD HECKE ALGEBRAS
KO HONDA, ROMAN KRUTOWSKI, YIN TIAN, AND TIANYU YUAN
ABSTRACT. In this paper, we study obstructions to smoothing nodal orbicurves with orbighosts mapped into
cyclic quotient singularities. As an application, we show, under some assumptions, that the orbifold Hecke
algebra of a complex global quotient orbifold [X/G] is isomorphic to the degree 0 cohomology of the A∞-
algebra of endomorphisms of a regular cotangent ﬁber T ∗
[x][X/G] regarded as an object of the bulk-deformed
wrapped Fukaya category of the orbifold T ∗[X/G] for a compact complex manifold X and ﬁnite group G ⊂
Aut(X).

KO HONDA, ROMAN KRUTOWSKI, YIN TIAN, AND TIANYU YUAN
1. INTRODUCTION
The aim of this paper is to study the orbifold Floer theory of a global quotient [M/G] for a sym-
plectic manifold (M, ω) together with an action of a ﬁnite subgroup G of its symplectomorphism group
Symp(M, ω). Speciﬁcally, we build tools for deﬁning reﬁned invariants such as reduced (open) Gromov-
Witten invariants and Fukaya categories. This usually requires a careful analysis of the moduli space of
stable curves, and, in particular, of the compactiﬁcation of the main stratum. One way to analyze an es-
sential part of the compactiﬁcation is to determine which orbicurves are not smoothable. In this work, we
focus on nodal twisted orbicurves with ghosts on cyclic quotient singularities and obtain obstructions to
smoothing such curves. We then use these obstructions to deﬁne and obtain some computations of wrapped
Fukaya categories of particular global quotient orbifolds.
In the non-orbifold Calabi-Yau setting, recent advances of Doan-Walpuski [DW23] and Ekholm-Shende
[ES25] enable us to deﬁne reduced (open) Gromov-Witten invariants of arbitrary genus. In both [DW23]
and [ES25], the authors obtain obstruction results to smoothings of closed curves with a ghost bubble of
positive genus attached at a single point. These results build on a long line of work [Zin09a; Zin09b; Niu16]
under certain assumptions on the genus, starting with the work of Ionel [Ion98]. On the algebro-geometric
side, these ideas were developed in low genus settings in [Vak00; Vak01]. The algebro-geometric analogs
of the obstruction results of [DW23; ES25] were sharpened in the paper of Rezaee-Swaminathan [RS26] to
the case of ghosts attached at multiple points, and [RS25] provided a criterion for smoothing nodal curves
with ghost bubbles. (Their symplectic analogs are known only in the lower-genus cases from the works of
[Zin09a; Niu16].) We also note that recently Xu [Xu26] obtained a deﬁnition of reduced Gromov-Witten
invariants using soft topological methods, avoiding the need to establish obstructions to smoothings.
Recall that an orbicurve
[u]: (C , q1, . . . , qb) →[M/G]
is a map from a stacky curve C , possibly with boundary,1 whose smooth stacky points qi are required to be
mapped to the singular locus of [M/G] (i.e., the locus of all stacky points). A sequence of smooth orbicurves
may converge to a nodal orbicurve with an orbighost, corresponding to a tuple of stacky points colliding on
the domain. Ruling out such orbighosts is highly nontrivial even in the case of genus 0 orbicurves.
In this paper, we initiate the study of obstructions to smoothings of orbicurves and obtain criteria for
ruling out orbighosts landing on cyclic quotient singularities; see Section 1.1 for more precise statements.
We then apply the smoothing obstructions developed in this paper to one important class of examples:
Let X be a complex manifold, equipped with a ﬁnite subgroup G of automorphisms. There is a naturally
induced action of G on M = T ∗X, and our aim is to deﬁne and obtain computations of the regular wrapped
Fukaya category of [T ∗X/G] bulk-deformed by inertia components of the inertia orbifold I[T ∗X/G], de-
noted Wreg
ℏ([T ∗X/G]), where ℏis the associated tuple of bulk parameters. The inertia components of
[T ∗X/G] are naturally in bijection with the inertia components of [X/G]. A connected (complex) hy-
persurface Y ⊂X ﬁxed by the G-action corresponds to an embedded copy of T ∗Y ⊂T ∗X, which is
ﬁxed by the induced action. This component is an example of a (compound) Am−1-singularity, i.e., locally
[T ∗X/G] near a generic point of [G · T ∗Y/G] is given by
[C2/µm] × Cn−2,
with the cyclic group µm of order m acting on C2 with opposite characters ζ±1
m = e±2πi/m. Using our ob-
struction results we deﬁne Wreg
ℏ([T ∗X/G]) bulk-deformed by these Am−1-singularities; see Theorem 3.18,
which proves a proposal from [MSS25b, Section 3.2] in this particular case.
More signiﬁcantly, we obtain a partial computation of the A∞-algebra of endomorphisms of a cotangent
ﬁber T ∗
[x][X/G] over a point [x] associated with a regular point x ∈Xreg. This computation is an extension
of the arguments of Abouzaid [Abo12] and Abbondandolo-Schwarz [AS06; AS10a] to the orbifold setting.
It involves counting orbidisks with boundary on the 0-section [X/G] ⊂[T ∗X/G], which is a singular
Lagrangian (by this we mean a Lagrangian with a nontrivial singular locus). To our knowledge, this is

1.1. Obstructions to smoothing nodal orbicurves. Let (M, ω) be a symplectic manifold equipped with
an ω-tame almost complex structure J0 and an effective action of a ﬁnite group G of symplectomorphisms
preserving J0. Consider a cyclic quotient singularity [G · Y/G] ⊂[M/G] arising from a connected
component Y of the ﬁxed point set M H, where H ⊂G is a cyclic subgroup isomorphic to the group
µm = {ζj
m | j = 0, . . . m −1} of mth roots of unity generated by ζm = e2πi/m, for some m ∈Z≥2. The
component Y is smooth and the normal bundle νY admits an isotypic decomposition
νY = ⊕χ∈IrrC(H)νχ,
(1.2)
where the action of H on the ﬁbers of the J0-complex subbundle νχ corresponds to a direct sum of copies
of the nontrivial irreducible C-representations of H corresponding to the character χ.
We will assume that J0 is adapted to the cyclic quotient singularity [G · Y/G] in the following sense:
Let Y◦⊂Y denote the immersed submanifold of points y ∈Y with the minimal possible stabilizer, i.e.,
with Gy = H. Then:
(1) an auxiliary choice of an open G-invariant neighborhood U of G · Y◦, together with a choice of
almost complex G-invariant immersed isotypic submanifolds Vχ ⊂U for χ ∈IrrC(H) containing
Y◦, such that
T Vχ|Y◦= T Y◦⊕νχ;
(2) a choice of G-equivariant J0-holomorphic projections
πχ : U →Vχ,
πχ|Vχ = id .
In Section 5.1 we show that such adapted almost complex structures J0 exist in abundance.
A pseudoholomorphic orbicurve [u]: (C , q1, . . . , qb) →[M/G], given by a map from a (potentially
nodal) twisted orbidisk C which is injective on isotropy groups, can be represented by a G-equivariant
J0-holomorphic curve
u: Σ →M,
with [Σ/G] = C (see Lemma 2.8), and moreover the projection π: Σ →C has (q1, . . . , qb) as its branching

the cover π: Σ →C, where (hi) ∈Conj(G) is the conjugacy class of an element of G ﬁxing a ramiﬁcation
point in π−1(qi). Hence, domains of the orbicurves can be viewed as elements of the Hurwitz moduli space

The following theorem is our main result on obstructing smoothings of orbicurves and is a combination
of Theorems 5.34 and 5.37.

By Theorem 1.1, once the obstruction space of 1-forms on the ghost curve [u ] has been determined to be
nonzero, the existence of a smoothing imposes additional constraints on the Taylor series of the main curve
u• at the places where the nodes are attached. The computation of the obstruction space for an arbitrary
cyclic quotient singularity reduces to an index calculation, which is somewhat tedious and depends on the
combinatorics of the stacky points on the ghost curve C .
In this paper, we are mainly interested in the case of (compound) Am−1-singularities, and for those

Remark 1.3. We make no assumptions on the genus of orbicurves in the proof of Theorem 1.1. On the
other hand, we use assumptions on the genus of the orbighost in our proof of Theorem 1.2.
Remark 1.4. The results are not immediate from the obstruction results of [DW23] and [ES25], even in
the simplest case H ∼= µ2. However, in the case of µ2-stabilizers, one does not need to require J to be
adapted to the singularity, since the expected leading term in the normal direction is linear. Our proof is an
upgraded version of the proof [DW23] to the equivariant setup. It would be interesting to see whether the
techniques of [ES25] are sufﬁcient to arrive at a similar conclusion.
Remark 1.5. We comment here on the applicability of Theorem 1.1 to deﬁning reduced invariants of gen-
eral cyclic quotient singularities. A particularly advantageous property of Am−1-singularities is that the
index of [u•] coincides with the index of any curve in the sequence [uk], or in other words, [u ] gives
a dimensionless contribution. For general cyclic quotient singularities, though, the index of u
may be
negative, and we need to introduce virtual perturbations. If both dimensionless and ghosts of negative
dimensions are expected to appear in the Gromov compactiﬁcation, one would have to choose virtual per-
turbations carefully in order to apply our Theorem 1.1. This is a promising future venue to pursue, and the
only work that explores this kind of idea that we are aware of is [ES24].
1.2. Wrapped Fukaya category of global quotient orbifolds and Hecke algebras. Let X be a complex
manifold and G ⊂Aut(X) be a ﬁnite subgroup. We denote by Xreg ⊂X the regular locus of points

hypersurface with stabilizer HY ⊂G, which is necessarily isomorphic to µmY for some mY > 1. To each
G-orbit of Y , we associate formal variables ℏY

The insight of generalizing the quadratic Hecke relation to the higher degree relation of the form (1.10)

## Page 6


6
KO HONDA, ROMAN KRUTOWSKI, YIN TIAN, AND TIANYU YUAN
complex reﬂection groups. Many examples of Hecke algebras of orbifolds, arising from actions with non-
real reﬂections, are generalizations of the Hecke algebras of complex reﬂection groups.
Etingof’s deﬁnition of the orbifold Hecke algebras can be viewed as a far-reaching generalization of
Cherednik’s original topological interpretation of the double afﬁne Hecke algebra of type A1 [Che05]. It
provides a uniﬁed way of deﬁning Hecke algebras which appear in representation theory. Among the ex-
amples of orbifold Hecke algebras are afﬁne Hecke algebras and double afﬁne Hecke algebras of connected
simple Lie groups, Hecke algebras of complex reﬂection groups of Brou´e-Malle-Rouquier [BMR98], and
also generalized double afﬁne Hecke algebras considered in [EGO06] and [EOR07]. We review these and
other examples in Section 7, and we also refer the reader to [Eti17, Section 3.7].
In this paper, we deﬁne the regular wrapped Fukaya category Wreg
ℏ(T ∗[X/G]) of the cotangent bundle
orbifold [T ∗X/G] with the induced action of G, bulk-deformed by components of the inertia orbifold
I[T ∗X/G] associated with AmY −1-singularities T ∗Y ⊂T ∗X, where Y is a connected hypersurface. An
object L of this category is a disjoint G-orbit of an embedded, cylindrical-at-inﬁnity Lagrangian L ⊂T ∗X,
which can also be treated as a Lagrangian in [T ∗X/G], avoiding the singular locus. We denote by T ∗
[x][X/G]
the G-orbit of a ﬁber T ∗
xX at x ∈Xreg. We denote by HW ∗
ℏ(T ∗
[x][X/G]) the graded algebra associated
with the A∞-algebra of endomorphisms of T ∗
[x][X/G] in Wreg
ℏ(T ∗[X/G]). We show:
Theorem 1.6 (= Theorem 6.6 and Corollary 6.7). There exists a surjective morphism
EV : HW 0
ℏ(T ∗
[x][X/G]) →Hℏ|ℏ0=0([X/G]).
If π2(X) ⊗Q = 0, the morphism EV is an isomorphism. In particular, if X is a K(π, 1), the graded
algebra HW ∗
ℏ(T ∗
[x][X/G]) is concentrated in degree 0, and therefore the orbifold Hecke algebra gives the
full computation of HW ∗
ℏ(T ∗
[x][X/G]).
The proof of Theorem 1.6 is based on an analysis of the boundary degenerations in the moduli spaces
of stacky half-strips, with the requirement that the outgoing end of such a half-strip is mapped to the 0-
section (here we are using Abouzaid’s terminology [Abo11]). Using Theorem 1.2 we rule out all boundary
degenerations except for the one corresponding to the formation of a nodal orbidisk with a unique stacky
point. These degenerations give rise to the Hecke relation (1.9).
Let us conclude the introduction with some remarks on Theorem 1.6:
Remark 1.7. Theorem 1.6 signiﬁcantly extends the scope of the main theorem of [HTY26], which also
follows from the results of [HKTY25]. In [CHT25; HKTY25], the authors deﬁned an analog of our bulk-
deformed orbifold wrapped Fukaya category in the special case of symmetric products. The approach
used — which goes back to the work of Lipshitz [Lip06] — is the cylindrical reformulation of the orbifold
wrapped Fukaya category for symmetric products. This is based on the classical observation [ACV03,
Proposition 4.2.2, Theorem 4.3.2] that the stack of twisted Sk-covers is equivalent to the properly deﬁned
stack of admissible k-fold branched covers. The same idea is used in [KY23] to deﬁne a version of sym-
plectic cohomology for symmetric products.
Remark 1.8. The classical references for orbifold Gromov-Witten invariants and orbifold Floer theory
in the symplectic literature include [CR02; CP14]. Recently, several groups of authors have developed
the foundational aspects further, among which we note [GZ21; MSS25b]. There is also ongoing work by
McLean and Ritter aimed at developing a framework for orbifold Gromov-Witten theory based on global
Kuranishi charts. There have been several recent developments and applications in orbifold Gromov-Witten
and Floer theories, which include [ACHL24; PS23b; FLYZ25; MS21a].
Remark 1.9. It would be interesting to see whether Theorem 1.6 can be proved using virtual methods. It is
asserted in [MSS25a] that one can deﬁne the regular (wrapped) Fukaya category of orbifolds by combining
their ideas together with [HH25] and [Rab24]. However, performing the computation provided by Theo-
rem 6.6 requires working with a 0-section, which is a singular Lagrangian, and the methods of these papers
alone do not sufﬁce. Recently, Xu [Xu26] obtained a deﬁnition of reduced closed Gromov-Witten invari-
ants by using a new type of stratiﬁed transversality result, which is similar in spirit to FOP transversality


## Page 7


SMOOTHING ORBICURVES AND HECKE ALGEBRAS
7
developed in [BX22]. This at least suggests that one may attempt to reprove Theorem 1.6 using virtual
methods combined with stratiﬁed transversality methods, which may be helpful for generalizations of such
computations to other quotient singularities.
Remark 1.10. Theorem 1.6 only gives a computation in degree 0. The work [HKTY25] suggests how one
could build a Morse chain model computing such an A∞-algebra by using Morse theory on the space of
G-paths. It is still unclear whether such a Morse-theoretic approach provides a way to compute this A∞-
algebra in many cases of interest. Therefore, a robust deﬁnition of such a derived orbifold Hecke algebra
is warranted, and one may anticipate methods of string topology to provide an efﬁcient way of computing
such an algebra.
Organization of the paper. In Section 2 we introduce some standard notions from the general theory of orb-
ifolds and orbifold Floer theory, and in Section 3 we deﬁne the wrapped Fukaya category of T ∗[X/G]. In
Section 4 we introduce the notion of equivariant J-holomorphicjets, and establish the necessary transversal-
ity results for maps evaluating jets of equivariant curves at the preimages of the stacky points. Theorem 1.1
is proved in Section 5 and Theorem 1.6 is proved in Section 6. In Section 7, we provide several examples
for which the isomorphism of Theorem 6.6 recovers the orbifold Hecke algebras in terms of the wrapped
Fukaya category.

2. BASIC NOTIONS FOR GLOBAL QUOTIENT ORBIFOLDS
In this section, we give a brief review of some topological and geometric aspects of orbifolds, focusing
mainly on global quotients. We expect the reader’s familiarity with the general theory of orbifolds and
orbifolds with corners, and we usually understand an orbifold X as a stack over the category of smooth
manifolds (with corners) which admits an atlas U →X, with the associated groupoid U ×X U ⇒U being
a proper ´etale Lie groupoid; see [Ler10, Remark 4.33]. For more details on the general theory of orbifolds,
differentiable stacks and orbispaces, we refer the reader to [Ler10; ALR07; MM03; ST24; Zer17; Met03;
BX11; Joy17a; Hen05; Par22]. We note, however, that for global quotient orbifolds, most of the paper can
be understood from the simpler equivariant perspective.
2.1. Some deﬁnitions and examples. We use the notation [X/G] for the global quotient orbifold asso-
ciated to an n-dimensional manifold X with an action of a ﬁnite group G. Given x ∈X, we denote its
stabilizer by Gx ⊂G. We also use the notation Xg to denote the ﬁxed point set of an element g ∈G. We

isotropy. As notation suggests, we treat these points as singular. Nevertheless, these points are smooth,
since they admit orbifold charts in [X/G], and we also refer to them as smooth stacky points.

2.2. Maps from orbicurves. In this paper, we are concerned with smooth orbifold maps
[u]: C →[M/G],

to [Taa25] for a detailed introduction to orbicurves in the context of algebraic geometry and to [CP14]
for a discussion of orbidisks from the perspective of smooth topology. All orbicurves and orbidisks under
consideration are orientable, and hence the main difference between them and nodal curves/disks is the
potential presence of stacky points, i.e., points with nontrivial isotropy groups.
The stacky points are either interior stacky points with a local chart of the form [C/µm] for some m > 0,
or balanced interior stacky nodes with a local chart of the form

The balancing condition, in fact, makes a nodal orbidisk into a twisted nodal orbidisk, and we only consider
twisted orbidisks. Notice that there is no need to consider stacky points on the boundary of an orbidisk
since we are only interested in orientable orbidisks.
All smooth nodal maps (2.12) that we study in this paper are assumed to be injective on isotropy groups,
i.e., the associated maps on isotropy groups are assumed to be injective. In particular, any such u maps
stacky points of C to singular points of [M/G].
Let us assume for a moment that a map [u] comes from an honest functor between proper ´etale Lie
groupoids representing these orbifolds
U : [C1 ⇒C0] →[M × G ⇒M],
i.e., U is a functor between these two categories. Then injectivity on isotropy groups for U is equivalent
to its faithfulness. However, orbifold morphisms do not always come from morphisms of Lie groupoids on
the nose; see [GZ21, Section 2.1].
As observed in [MSS25b], we may interpret a smooth nodal curve that is injective on isotropy groups in

2.3. Remark on representability. We recall that a morphism
f : X →Y
of stacks over a site S (which in our case is the category Man of smooth manifolds) is representable if for
any Y ∈S and any morphism Y →Y the ﬁbered product stack X ×Y Y is representable by an object
of S . Here, by the standard abuse of notation, we denote by Y the stack Mor(−, Y ) over S associated
with Y .
When S is the category Top of topological spaces, a map f : X →Y between two orbispaces is
representable if and only if it is injective on isotropy groups by [Par22, Corollary 3.6]. It is well-known,
however, that Man is not closed under ﬁber products, and hence a smooth map between two smooth mani-
folds f : M →N is representable if and only if f is a submersion; see e.g., [Met03, Lemma 71]. Therefore,
one should not expect mere injectivity on isotropy groups to be sufﬁcient for representability.
Nonetheless, there is a notion of weak representability, in which Y →Y is assumed to be an atlas
instead of an arbitrary morphism; see [BX11, Deﬁnition 2.34]. The generalization of [ALR07, Theorem
2.45] then states that a morphism of orbifolds injective on isotropy groups is weakly representable if and
only if it is injective on isotropy groups. This statement, which was observed in [BX11, Remark 2.37],
follows almost immediately from [Joy17b, Theorem 11.39], since any atlas is a submersion in the sense of


## Page 11


SMOOTHING ORBICURVES AND HECKE ALGEBRAS
11
[BX11, Deﬁnition 2.5]. The main observation here is that the ﬁbered product of transversal morphisms of
orbifolds is again an orbifold (for a concise discussion of this fact see [GZ21, Section 2.1.3]), and hence in
our case X ×Y Y is an orbifold since Y →Y is a submersion. Then computation of the isotropy groups
in [Joy17b, Theorem 11.39], which is equivalent in this case to the proof of [ALR07, Theorem 2.45], shows
that isotropy groups of X ×Y Y are trivial, implying that this orbifold is representable by a manifold.
We also note that this lack of ﬁber products is the reason why one may wish to work with the cate-
gory of C∞-schemes, and as [Joy17a, Theorem 7.21] shows, the equivalence between representability and
injectivity on isotropy groups holds for stacks in this category.
Historically, in orbifold Floer theory, smooth maps injective on isotropy groups are called representable
after [CR02]. As our discussion above indicates, this name is not satisfactory from the stacky perspective.
3. WRAPPED FUKAYA CATEGORY OF A GLOBAL QUOTIENT ORBIFOLD
3.1. Geometric preliminaries for Fukaya categories of orbifolds. In this paper, we are mainly interested
in studying global quotients of Liouville manifolds, but we emphasize that most of the results of this sec-
tion are applicable in a more general setting. The following geometric discussion is an adaptation of the
orientation package of Seidel [Sei08, Section 11] to the equivariant setting. Our discussion is similar in
spirit to [BH21; CH17; CCJ20].
Recall that a Liouville manifold is a pair (M, λ) consisting of a manifold M and a Liouville 1-form λ on
M such that ω = dλ is symplectic and, outside of a compact region, (M, λ) is identiﬁed with a cylindrical
end ([1, +∞)r × ∂M, rα), where α is a contact form on ∂M.
Assumptions on (M, λ), G and L. Let (M, λ) be a Liouville manifold and G a ﬁnite group acting on M.
We assume the following:
(A1) the action of G on M preserves λ and the action of G on the cylindrical end is induced from the
action of G on ∂M; and
(A2) c1(M) = 0 and there is a choice of a G-invariant complex volume form η on M with respect to
some compatible G-invariant almost complex structure.
Then there is an associated phase map αM : Gr+(T M) →S1 given by
v1 ∧· · · ∧vn 7→η(v1 ∧· · · ∧vn)
|v1 ∧· · · ∧vn| ,
for any choice of oriented basis (v1, . . . , vn) for a given oriented Lagrangian in the oriented Lagrangian
Grassmannian Gr+(T M).
We further assume that any Lagrangian submanifold L ⊂M under consideration satisﬁes the following:
(A3) L is exact, that is, there exists a function fL : L →R such that λ|L = dfL;
(A4) L is cylindrical at inﬁnity, that is, there exists a Legendrian submanifold Λ of ∂M such that L takes
the form [1, ∞) × Λ on the cylindrical end [1, ∞) × ∂M.
It is well-known that with the above assumptions (A1)–(A4), one can deﬁne the wrapped Fukaya cate-
gory over Z; we refer the reader to [Sei08; Sei00] for more details.
An equivariant Lagrangian is a G-invariant collection L of oriented Lagrangian submanifolds of M; by
abuse of notation, we also write L for the union of its elements.
Remark 3.1. Although L itself is not necessarily a Lagrangian submanifold of M, the suborbifold [L/G] ⊂
[M/G] can be given a structure of a Lagrangian suborbifold of the symplectic orbifold [M/G] in the sense
of [COW24]. The simplest example is the orbit of a line passing through the origin under the standard
rotation action of G = µm on M = R2. This orbit is an equivariant Lagrangian, but only immersed in R2.
The tangent space of an equivariant Lagrangian L is a collection of tangent spaces to Lagrangians that
belong to L, and, at a point p ∈L with stabilizer Gp, the tangent space to L is identiﬁed with a |Gp|-tuple
of Lagrangian subspaces of TpM related by the Gp-action.
The main example of interest to us is the following:

3.3.1. Hurwitz moduli spaces. A Hurwitz datum is an ordered tuple h = ((h1), . . . , (hb)) of b elements
(hi) ∈Conj(G), for some b ∈Z>0. Let H G
0,h,d be the rigidiﬁed stack of smooth admissible G-covers
(π: Σ →C; q1, . . . , qb)
of a disk with d + 1 boundary punctures, ordered counterclockwise — the 0th puncture is negative, and
the rest are positive — whose Hurwitz datum is h. By this we mean (hi) is the conjugacy class of an
element of Gp for p ∈π−1(qi) acting via ζ|Gp| on TpΣ. This is also given by the conjugacy class of a
deck transformation of Σ corresponding to a small loop around qi and hence is sometimes called the local
monodromy around qi.
We assume that d + 2b ≥2, which implies that the marked curves C are stable. Since we consider
the rigidiﬁed stack, the stabilizers are trivial, and this stack is representable by a smooth manifold. The
forgetful map
H G
0,h,d →Rd,b
is a smooth ﬁnite cover, where Rd,b is the moduli space of (d + 1)-punctured disks with b interior marked
points.
We always denote by C the orbidisk associated to (π: Σ →C; q1, . . . , qb), which has q1, . . . , qb as its
smooth stacky points, and we treat C as the coarse curve of C .
Remark 3.5. The non-rigidiﬁed stack decomposes into components corresponding to elements of the set of
conjugacy classes of subgroups of G, corresponding to the image H in G of the orbifold fundamental group
π1(C ) under the monodromy action. Such an image in particular determines the number of connected
components |G|/|H| in the arbitrary cover in the given component. The group of global automorphisms of
this component is isomorphic to the centralizer CG(H) of H in G (see [Gal22, Proposition 2.12]) and is
quotiented out upon rigidiﬁcation. In particular, for the substack of connected G-covers, one quotients out
of the action of the centralizer Z(G). For more details on rigidiﬁcation, we refer the reader to [ACV03,
Section 5].
We denote by H G
0,h,∅= H G

Deligne-Mumford stack M0,2b+(d+1) contained in the locus ﬁxed by the anti-complex involution. This
doubling extends to the doubling of the whole G-cover, and one obtains an element of H
G
(0,d+1),h∪h of
the stack of admissible G-covers with (d + 1) marked (non-stacky) points downstairs and Hurwitz datum
h ∪h, where local monodromies in h are obtained by inverting those of h.
□

be mapped to T ∗Z which is a symplectic submanifold of W of real codimension ≥2. This imposes an

4. TRANSVERSALITY FOR EQUIVARIANT JET EVALUATION
Let M be a smooth manifold equipped with a G-equivariant almost complex structure J. For a G-
equivariant J-holomorphic curve u: Σ →M at a point p ∈Σ with a nontrivial stabilizer, the equivariance
itself usually imposes a strong vanishing condition of coefﬁcients in its Taylor series. For instance, a
holomorphic curve representing a morphism of orbifolds
[C/µm] →[C/µm],
with actions by characters χm
1 and χm
k on the domain and codomain, has a Taylor expansion of the form
u(z) =
∞
X
j=0
ck+jmzk+jm.
For this reason, the mere vanishing of the ﬁrst or even second derivative does not prevent a certain curve u
from appearing in the moduli space of G-equivariant curves of a ﬁxed dimension.
The goal of this section is to systematically study equivariant J-holomorphic jets, extending the ideas
of [Zeh15], and show that the subspace of curves with vanishing of the ﬁrst nontrivial equivariant J-
holomorphic jet is a submanifold of positive codimension.
4.1. Equivariant J-holomorphic jets. Let G be a ﬁnite group and let (M, J) be an almost complex
manifold with a J-holomorphic G-action. Consider a subgroup H ⊂G, its ﬁxed point submanifold M H,
and a connected component Y ⊂M H. This submanifold is almost complex, since G preserves the almost
complex structure, and, moreover, we have an isotypic splitting
T M|Y ∼=

each ﬁber of νρ as an H-representation is a sum of ﬁnitely many copies of ρ. Observe that T Y corresponds
to the trivial representation. We mainly focus on the case of a cyclic subgroup H ∼= µm, and we assume
that H is an essential subgroup, i.e., the stabilizer of a generic point of Y is exactly H = ⟨g⟩for some
generator g ∈H.

Let us recall the notion of J-holomorphic jets Jk,J(D, M) of smooth curves.
Deﬁnition 4.1. We say that two J-holomorphic maps u1, u2: D →M such that u1(z0) = u2(z0) = x are
k-jet equivalent at z0 ∈D if for any smooth curve γ : R →D with γ(0) = z0 the curves u1 ◦γ and u2 ◦γ
have kth order of contact at 0, i.e., for any smooth function ϕ: M →R the difference
ϕ ◦u1 ◦γ −ϕ ◦u2 ◦γ
has ﬁrst k derivatives vanishing at 0 ∈R.
A J-holomorphic k-jet at (z0, x) ∈D × M is an equivalence class of J-holomorphic maps u: D →M
with u(z0) = x under k-jet equivalence relation.
We refer the reader to [KMS93, Section 12] for a detailed introduction to jets of smooth maps. Let
Jk(D, M) be the set of standard k-jets, which is known to be a smooth ﬁber bundle over D × M, and let

4.2. Transversality.
4.2.1. Transversality for strips. In this section, we assume the setup of Section 3.1, and work under the
assumption M = T ∗X, while keeping the discussion sufﬁciently general.
We ﬁrst show that there is a choice of a family Jt, t ∈[0, 1], with each Jt ∈J such that the moduli
spaces M(ˆx1, ˆx0; ∅) are transversely cut out for all choices of ˆx1 and ˆx0. Observe that H0,∅,1 consists of
just one point corresponding to the trivial G-cover over a strip.
Lemma 4.8. There exists a family Jt, t ∈[0, 1], such that all moduli spaces M(ˆx1, ˆx0; ∅) are transversely
cut out.
Sketch of proof. First observe that we only consider pairs (L, L′) of regular equivariant Lagrangians, hence
curves in M(ˆx1, ˆx0; ∅) for any pair ˆx1, ˆx0 ∈χ(L, L′) are not contained in the ﬁxed point set of any
subgroup of G. Any such curve corresponds to a G-orbit of a Jt-holomorphic strip connecting a ﬁxed
Hamiltonian chord x ∈ˆx0 to an arbitrary chord y ∈ˆx1. The statement of the lemma in the case G = µ2
then essentially follows from [KS02, Proposition 5.13]. The same proof can be repeated for an arbitrary
ﬁnite group G, and we omit the details. We also refer the reader to [Mor19, Theorem 5.2.7], which treats
arbitrary G for Hamiltonian Floer homology.
□
4.2.2. Transversality for general G-equivariant curves. Consider the case h ̸= ∅or d > 2.
By the usual deformation theory, a neighborhood Uπ of (π: Σ →C; q1, . . . , qb) in H G
0,h,d is diffeomor-
phic to (Cb × Rd−2)y and is given by varying the almost complex structure on C away from the boundary
and the marked points (q1, . . . , qb), with j0 being the standard complex structure on the unit disk. These
almost complex structures naturally induce almost complex structures jy on Σ for y ∈Cb × Rd−2 within a
small neighborhood of the origin, and we regard Uπ as the set of such almost complex structures. Clearly,
this is identiﬁed with a variation of π∗j0 in the set of G-equivariant almost complex structures on Σ. The
universal curve SG
0,h,d admits a trivialization over Uπ, i.e.,
SG
0,h,d|Uπ ∼= Σ × Uπ.

equivariant Lagrangians L0, . . . , Ld. We also ﬁx a connected component m of I[M/G]b, consisting of

Proof. Let us denote by (π: Σ →C; q1, . . . , qb) the element of the Hurwitz moduli space associated with
any curve u under discussion. The fact that M is smooth is standard, and is a combination of the proof of
[CM07, Proposition 4.3] and the proof presented in [Sei08, Section (9k)]. Let us brieﬂy spell out the main
difference: since we only use the perturbation of an almost complex structure, in order to apply a unique
continuation type argument, we need to be able to show that the term of the form
Z ◦(du −X ˜
H ⊗˜α) ◦jy
can be picked to be sufﬁciently arbitrary on some open subset of the domain Σ in order to use it to approxi-
mate a Dirac δ-type section. Since h ̸= ∅or d > 2, the curve u is nonconstant on an open dense subset, and
by intersecting this open set with the preimage of the auxiliary open subset U ⊂C where α vanishes (see
Deﬁnition 3.9), we obtain the open subset where the above form takes shape Z ◦du ◦jy, with Z arbitrary,
hence can be used for the above-mentioned purposes.
Since the action of G on X , and hence on M , is smooth, the ﬁxed point set M G of the restriction of
this action on M is a smooth submanifold. The description of the tangent spaces follows immediately since
DX is the linearization of the section s along M . The proof of the ﬁrst surjectivity claim in (2) is also
given in the proof of [CM07, Proposition 4.3], and the surjectivity of the restriction to the G-invariant parts
follows by averaging once we establish the equivariance of DX .

Remark 4.11. The proof of equivariance can also be carried out using an extension of the global expression
for Du,y from [MS04, Proposition 3.1.1] by utilizing the Levi-Civita connection induced by an equivariant
metric on M. One can avoid direct computations entirely, since the above is a variation of an equivariant
section s, but we ﬁnd this direct computation illustrative.
Remark 4.12. Equivariant transversality of this kind has been studied before. Besides the already men-
tioned [Mor19], the case of closed curves was considered in [Rot19]. In particular, [Rot19, Theorem 4.3]
shows that equivariant transversality can be achieved by picking a generic G-invariant almost complex
structure on M which is not domain-dependent.

5. OBSTRUCTIONS TO SMOOTHING CURVES WITH GHOSTS ON SINGULARITIES
5.1. Regularization near the ﬁxed points. We ﬁrst introduce the notion of an almost complex structure
that is adapted to the singular locus.
Deﬁnition 5.1. A G-equivariant almost complex structure on the Liouville manifold M is adapted to a
collection {(Yi, (gi))}i∈I of inertia components of I[M/G] if the following hold:

The goal of this subsection is to prove that such almost complex structures exist in the case of our main
interest, i.e., for the type A inertia components associated with T ∗Y ⊂T ∗X, for X a complex manifold
equipped with an action of a ﬁnite group G ⊂Aut(X), and Y is an element of the collection of the ﬁxed
hypersurfaces of this action.
First, we pick a Hermitian G-invariant metric gX on X. For each connected hypersurface Y ⊂Xsing

πY : νY →Y
orthogonal to T Y , which is also the only nontrivial isotypic component of T X|Y . This subbundle provides
a natural embedding of T ∗Y as a subset
Y ={(y, p) ∈T ∗X | y ∈Y, p(x) = 0 for x ∈νY }.

Remark 5.3. This proof shows that compatibility is out of reach since ω and R∇ν are of completely different
natures. In the study of relative invariants with respect to a symplectic divisor, some miracles occur due
to which one is able to pick the curvature of type (1, 1) with respect to an adapted J as in [PS24, Remark
6.2.6]. Our Liouville submanifold T ∗Y is of codimension 4, so there is no reason for such miracles. This
is the main reason for working with tame almost complex structures in the context of orbifold Floer theory.

Now that we understand how to construct an adapted almost complex structure on a δY-neighborhood
of T ∗Y inside T ∗|νY |, it only remains to build a G-equivariant almost complex structure on the whole
manifold M.
To construct such an almost complex structure, for each G-orbit G · Y of any such hypersurface Y , we
pick a function εY : Y →R≥0 satisfying the following:
(i) εY is positive on Y ◦and vanishes on ∂Y ◦, where Y ◦⊂Y is the maximal stratum of the ﬁxed
point stratiﬁcation, i.e., Y ◦= {y ∈Y | Gy = H};
(ii) the images of εY -neighborhoods of the 0-sections of νY under jY ’s do not intersect away from
Xsing.
We deﬁne a Hermitian metric egX on X via an interpolation between gX and the pushforward of g|νY | in the
εY -neighborhood away from the εY /2-neighborhood for each hypersurface Y . This metric is automatically
G-equivariant.
Finally, setting δY = εY /2 we deﬁne eJ by interpolating between the almost complex structure induced
on M by egX and pushforwards of JY’s as explained above.
The construction we just presented proves the following:
Proposition 5.5. The cotangent bundle M = T ∗X of a complex manifold X admits an almost complex
structure J0 adapted to all type A inertia components. Moreover, we can assume that such J0 agrees with
the standard almost complex structure Jstd induced by the metric away from the type A inertia, and is given
by J0 = Jstd exp(Z), for ∥Z∥C0 < ε for any ﬁxed small ε > 0.
Remark 5.6. Proposition 5.5 generalizes to the case of a ﬁxed component Y ⊂X with a cyclic stabilizer
of a generic point, i.e., to a cyclic quotient singularity case. In this case, one simply needs to split νY into
isotypic components and repeat our constructions from above.

Remark 5.7. For future applications, one may want to generalize Proposition 5.5 to arbitrary collections
of inertia components in arbitrary Liouville G-manifolds, and we expect such a result to hold true. One may
also want to pick the auxiliary isotypic directions in a compatible way, and one should be able to achieve
this using the methods developed in [BX22, Appendix A].
5.2. Kuranishi model. In this subsection, we describe the gluing model for an orbicurve with a ghost
bubble, closely following the approach of [DW23]. We also describe the setup of Theorem 1.1 in more
detail. Our main applications concern the case where (M, ω) is a Liouville domain, but the arguments
below apply to an arbitrary symplectic manifold.
Let u0 : Σ0 →M be a G-equivariant curve equipped with some domain-dependent equivariant almost
complex structure J and (π: Σ0 →C0; q1, . . . , qb) a G-cover in the Hurwitz moduli space H
G

We assume that
C0 = (C• ⊔C , ν)
(5.17)
is a twisted nodal orbidisk, consisting of two components and a unique node ¨n = (n•, n ) with n∗∈C∗.
The component C
is either an orbidisk or an orbisphere. In the orbisphere case, ¨n is an interior node; it
may be stacky, and if so, is balanced. In the orbidisk case, ¨n is a standard boundary node.
As in [DW23, Section 2], we treat nodal orbicurves as smooth orbicurves equipped with a nodal structure
ν (or ˆν), i.e., an involution of the normalization of a nodal orbicurve which identiﬁes the preimages of a
given node and is the identity elsewhere. In this paradigm, each node is a pair of points on the curve, and
we use the notation ¨n to stress this. Sometimes we treat ¨n as a common point of C• and C , which is in
fact a point of the underlying quotient C0/ν.
We assume that the image of C
is given by a point in [G · Y/G], where [G · Y/G] is a cyclic quotient
singularity. In particular, the stabilizer H = HY of the symplectic submanifold Y is a cyclic subgroup of
order mY. We require the stabilizer of this point to be exactly HY, i.e., this point stays away from deeper
strata of the ﬁxed point stratiﬁcation. This implies that the order of the stabilizer of any stacky point z ∈C
divides mY. We denote by m¨n the order of the stabilizer of ¨n.
We ﬁx an Y-adapted ω-tame almost complex structure J0 in the sense of Deﬁnition 5.1 (2) and (3)
(we note that the contact-type condition is not required in the proof of obstruction results). Hence, for each
character χ ∈IrrC(H) for which the isotypic component νχ ⊂νY is nontrivial, we have an auxiliary choice
of a G-invariant almost complex submanifold Vχ. Here νχ is regarded as a J0-complex H-equivariant
bundle over Y.
The domain-dependent J on Σ0 is assumed to coincide with J0 near the branch points and on all closed
components. Moreover, we assume that J is in fact a domain-dependent almost complex structure over
the Hurwitz moduli space, which varies continuously in the C∞-topology, and we require the difference
|J −J0|C0 to be sufﬁciently small.
We assume that the boundary ∂Σ0 is mapped to a prescribed tuple of regular equivariant Lagrangians
intersecting transversely, and boundary punctures are mapped to G-orbits of intersection points. We will
assume that, for the metric on M that we ﬁx, all Lagrangians under consideration are totally geodesic. We
also note that we allow non-regular equivariant Lagrangians as well.
Remark 5.8. Although the current setup slightly differs from that of Section 3.1, we can reduce to the
current setup using the localization tricks of Section 5.3. One, of course, could rewrite the following gluing
analysis in the presence of full Floer data, but we prefer to work with simpliﬁed notation and estimates.

5.3. Localization tricks. Our ultimate goal is to extract information on the leading terms of the Taylor
expansion in the isotypic directions of the main component of a limit curve that has a ghost bubble. In the
case of an interior ghost formation, i.e., when a ghost is attached at an interior node, one would like to be
able to project the curves in the sequence onto the isotypic submanifold Vχ in order to “isolate” the leading
order term corresponding to this isotypic direction, which cannot be done globally. To remedy this, we
replace a given sequence of curves with “localized” curves on the domain without changing the behavior
near the nodes where the ghost is attached.
Remark 5.26. [ES25, Theorem 1.5] requires the curves in the sequence to be pseudoholomorphic only
on the portions of domains corresponding to ghost formation. Our localization tricks may be viewed as a
bridge from the “global on the domain” pseudoholomorphicity requirement in [DW23, Theorem 1.1] to this
“local on the domain” condition of Ekholm-Shende.

Σ0 = (Σ• ⊔Σ , ˆν) and the restriction of u∞to Σ
is a ghost curve attached at the interior nodes. Let
x = u∞(ΣmY) ∈Y
and let Uχ be an H-equivariant Darboux chart of πχ(x) in Vχ.

Remark 5.28. Before we give a proof, we would like to point out that curves euk may have nontrivial
obstructions for the linearized Cauchy-Riemann operator, including the curve eu∞, and the moduli space
M([Uχ/H], [Lχ/H], m′; eJ) is only guaranteed to be an orbispace near eu∞. However, our gluing setup
allows for such components in the obstruction space; see (5.25). In Theorem 5.34 below, we obtain a result
on the vanishing of the jets of eu∞at the nodes on the side of the non-ghost components, which clearly
implies the same statement for the initial curve u∞, which may belong to a smooth manifold, i.e., a zero-set
of some transverse section. This allows for dimension-counting arguments, despite the lack of transversality
for the localized curves.

The construction of the curves euk implies (1)–(3) immediately. Our next task is to prove (4) (and hence

Remark 5.29. The Lagrangian Lχ we constructed in the proof is not totally geodesic with respect to the
standard metric. Nevertheless, in the pregluing one can instead use a metric which coincides with the metric
induced by eJ0 away from Lχ, and for which Lχ is totally geodesic. This does not affect any of the gluing
lemmas that we use.
5.3.2. Localization trick for boundary ghosts. In this subsection, we state and sketch the localization trick
for boundary ghosts. Unlike in the previous case, where the reason for localization was forced on us by
the necessity of projecting onto isotypic components, our motivation here is simply to avoid dealing with a
slightly more involved gluing setup, which involves Hamiltonian data and time-shifting maps.
As in the interior case, Y is a connected component of XH, where the cyclic group H is the stabilizer
of a generic point of Y. Let L be a connected Lagrangian preserved by the action of H and x ∈Y ∩L′,
where L′ is a connected component of LH. Since J0 is ω-tame, we have
νY ∼= νL′ ⊗R C,
(5.69)
where νL′ is the normal bundle for L′ ⊂L, treated as a real H-bundle over L′. We assume that νL′ does
not have any vectors of eigenvalue (−1), and therefore can be treated as a complex H-bundle.

Remark 5.33. Two important ingredients help make the above proof work.
(i) Working in the Darboux chart Uχ for the “isotypic direction” allows us to express the leading term
in the Taylor expansion in terms of the character χ.
(ii) The condition on η to be in the χ-subrepresentation guarantees that the leading term for the L2-
pairing is dictated by the leading terms of uσ,κ on the non-ghost side.

Remark 5.35. The proof of [DW23, Proposition 5.36] relies on the fact that the metric used in gluing comes
from the initial almost Hermitian structure, but only on the neck regions. It is used to show the containment
of the necks within tiny balls. If for pregluing we use a metric which coincides with the initial metric on a
ball of ﬁxed size centered at the image of the ghost, these energy arguments, based on [MS04, Section 4.7],
go unchanged.
5.4.2. Boundary ghosts. In this subsection, we work in the setup of Section 5.2.3, where we consider the
formation of a ghost attached at a boundary node, and our goal is to obtain restricting conditions on the jet
of u• at n•. Since ¨n is a regular, non-stacky boundary node, this condition involves only the ﬁrst derivative,
as equivariance imposes no vanishing constraint on this derivative.
That is to say, the present problem is simpler than the case of the interior ghost formation, considered in
the previous section. In particular, we do not need to project onto the isotypic directions.

6. ORBIFOLD HECKE ALGEBRAS AND THE A∞-ALGEBRAS OF COTANGENT FIBERS
6.1. Orbifold Hecke algebras. In this section, we recall Etingof’s orbifold Hecke algebras for complex
global quotient orbifolds, closely following [Eti17, Section 3].
We brieﬂy review G-paths and the orbifold fundamental group of a global quotient orbifold [X/G]. For
more details, we refer the reader to the classical reference [Hae90], and to [AC23] for a modern exposition,
which treats the global quotient case speciﬁcally.
Let ˆI = ⊔g∈GIg, where Ig is a copy of I = [0, 1]. We denote by g · t the point in Ig corresponding

ˆγ : ˆI →X,

ˆγ(e · 0) = x.

Moreover, this map is an algebra morphism.
Proof. The proof of both assertions is based on the analysis of boundary degenerations for the moduli
spaces T (ˆx; mY ,h) with |ˆx| = −1 (for the ﬁrst assertion) and T (ˆx2, ˆx1; mY ,h) with |ˆx1| = |ˆx2| = 0 (for
the second assertion). It should be regarded as a glimpse of veriﬁcation that a map of the form EV provides
an A∞-functor into a full derived Hecke algebra, which we do not deﬁne in this paper.
To prove this proposition, it is enough to establish the relations:
EV ◦µ1 = ∂sing ◦EV,
(6.23)
EV · EV = EV ◦µ2.
(6.24)
Let us note that the right-hand side of (6.23) involves evaluation map from CW 1
ℏ(T ∗
[x][X/G]) to C1(ΩG(Xreg, Gx); C)JℏK,
which apriori is not well-deﬁned since in a 1-parameter family a G-path not contained within Xreg can
occur. The analysis below shows, in particular, that such contributions cancel each other, making the right-
hand side well-deﬁned.
Speciﬁcally, we have the following list of boundary degenerations that do not involve ghosts for the
moduli spaces T (ˆx; mY ,h) and T (ˆx2, ˆx1; mY ,h):
M(ˆx, ˆx′; mY1,h1) × T (ˆx′; mY2,h2),
|ˆx| = 0, (Y , h) = ((Y1, h1), (Y2, h2));
(6.25)
M((ˆx2, ˆx1), ˆx′; mY1,h1) × T (ˆx′; mY2,h2),
|ˆx| = 0, (Y , h) = ((Y1, h1), (Y2, h2));
(6.26)
T (ˆx2; mY2,h2) × T (ˆx1; mY1,h1),
(Y , h) = ((Y1, h1), (Y2, h2)).
(6.27)
These contributions are standard, and we omit the detailed analysis of signs and gluing near these breakings
as it routinely follows by applying the results of Appendix A and Proposition 5.23.
The main challenge is to analyze ghost bubbling. Interior ghost bubbles can be ruled out as in the proof
of Theorem 3.18. The new difﬁculty is that some stacky points may approach the outgoing boundary ∂C
since the 0-section X is a singular Lagrangian, and a limit curve may admit boundary nodal ghosts. The

point cancel out using the Hecke relations. We notice that these degenerations behave similarly for both
T (ˆx; mY ,h) and T (ˆx2, ˆx1; mY ,h) as above, and we focus on the former.

7. EXAMPLES AND DISCUSSION
In this section, we discuss various examples of Hecke algebras in the literature and illustrate how Theo-
rem 6.6 allows one to recover these Hecke algebras in terms of the wrapped Fukaya category. The discussion
here is complementary to [Eti17, Section 3.7] and [Eti17, Section 4], to which we also refer our reader.
In Theorem 6.6 we work with cotangent bundles of global quotient orbifolds of the form [X/G], where
X is assumed to be compact. In Section 7.1 we list some of the well-known Hecke algebras that arise this
way.
In Section 7.2 we discuss examples which are associated with noncompact X. We note that such exam-
ples have appeared earlier historically.
7.1. Compact examples.
Example 7.1 (Double Afﬁne Hecke algebras). Let G be a simple Lie group, W be its Weyl group, and V
be the reﬂection representation of W. Let Q∨be the dual root lattice of W, and set Γ = Q∨⊕ηQ∨for
some η ∈C with Im(η) > 0. Then
X = V/Γ
is a compact complex torus. The algebra Hℏ([X/W]) is the double afﬁne Hecke algebra (DAHA) Hℏ(V, W)
of Cherednik [Che95]. This algebra is known to be ﬂat by the classical results of [Che95, Theorem 2.3],
[Sah99, Theorem 3.2]. It also follows from Etingof’s result since π2(X) = 0.
Corollary 6.7 applies in this case since X is a torus. Hence the whole graded algebra HW ∗
ℏ(T ∗
[x][X/W])
coincides with the Hecke algebra Hℏ|ℏ0=0([X/W]).
Example 7.2 (Cherednik algebras of complex crystallographic reﬂection groups). Let G be an irreducible
complex reﬂection group and V be its reﬂection representation. We assume that G is crystallographic, i.e.,
that there exists a cocompact lattice Γ ⊂V preserved by the action of G. We note that these groups were
classiﬁed by Popov [Pop82]; see also [Mal96] and [PS19, Section 3]. In particular, they include groups
G(ℓ, p, n) for ℓ= 1, 2, 3, 4 or 6 (and only for these values of ℓ∈Z≥1).
Since G preserves Γ, it induces an action on the compact complex torus X = V/Γ. The Hecke algebra
Hℏ([X/G]) was studied in [EM08, Section 6], [EFMV11] and [ACL25]. This example is a generalization
of Example 7.1, and by the same argument we have
HW ∗
ℏ(T ∗
[x][X/G]) ∼= HW 0
ℏ(T ∗
[x][X/G]) ∼= Hℏ|ℏ0=0([X/G]).
(7.1)


## Page 70


70
KO HONDA, ROMAN KRUTOWSKI, YIN TIAN, AND TIANYU YUAN
In the case of G(ℓ, 1, n) ∼= Sn ⋉(Z/ℓZ)n for ℓ= 2, 3, 4 or 6 this construction recovers the generalized
DAHAs of types D4, E6, E7 or E8, respectively, introduced in [EGO06].
Example 7.3 (Trigonometric analogs of symplectic reﬂection algebras). The trigonometric analog [Eti17,
Example 3.17] of a symplectic reﬂection algebra of [EG02] is given as follows: Let Λ be a symplectic
lattice, and G ⊂Sp(Λ) be a ﬁnite subgroup of its automorphisms. The action of G extends to the action
on V = R ⊗Z Λ, which admits a unitary G-invariant structure. Then G acts on the compact complex torus
X = V/Λ, and one obtains the associated Hecke algebra Hℏ([X/G]).
The isomorphism (7.1) also holds in this case. An interesting family of examples is given by the wreath
products Sn ⋉Γn acting on C2n, where Γ ⊂SL2(C) is a ﬁnite subgroup which preserves a rank 4 lattice.
For instance, one can take Γ to be the quaternion group Q8.
It is also well-known that by “doubling” a complex reﬂection group (V, G), which replaces the initial
representation with V ⊕V ∗, one obtains a symplectic reﬂection group. However, its Hecke algebra has no
deformation parameters, as there are no ﬁxed hypersurfaces.
Wreath products and doubled complex reﬂection groups constitute most of the indecomposable sym-
plectic reﬂection groups, and we refer the reader to [Coh80] for the classiﬁcation of symplectic reﬂection
groups, and [BS16, Section 4] and [Sch23] for a modern exposition.
Example 7.4 (Hecke algebras of orbifold surfaces). Let H be a simply connected Riemann surface, Γ ⊂
Aut(H) be a cocompact, discrete subgroup acting properly discontinuously with ﬁnite stabilizers, and Γ′ ⊂
Γ be the maximal ﬁnite index subgroup acting freely. Let X = Σ = H/Γ′ be the corresponding smooth
Riemann surface and G = Γ/Γ′. Then the Hecke algebra of the orbifold [H/Γ] is given by Hℏ([X/G]).
Let us note that any developable (or good) closed orbifold Riemann surface arises in this way.
In the case H = C or H = H+, this deformation is ﬂat by Theorem 6.4, and hence Theorem 6.6
provides the computation of the A∞-algebras of these orbifolds. These Hecke algebras were studied in
[ER05; EOR07].
The case H = X = P1 was studied in [ER08]. In particular, the corresponding Hecke algebra is not
ﬂat, and hence Theorem 6.6 does not apply here. We expect HW 0(T ∗
[x][X/G]) to be isomorphic to the
Hecke algebra, and moreover the differential in CW ∗(T ∗
[x][X/G]) to witness the non-ﬂatness of the Hecke
algebra Hℏ([X/G]). It would also be interesting to see the full computation of HW ∗(T ∗
[x][X/G]) for these
spherical orbifolds. The Morse-theoretic approach developed in [HKTY25] should provide sufﬁcient tools
for performing this computation.
Example 7.5 (Hecke algebra of symmetric powers of surfaces). Using H, Γ, Γ′, Σ from the previous exam-
ple, we consider X = Σn and G = Sn ⋉(Γ/Γ′)n. Then [X/G] = Symn([H/Γ]), and Theorem 6.6 again
applies if H ̸= P1.
In [HTY26], these algebras for Γ acting freely were called braid surface Hecke algebras. We note that
the braid surface Hecke algebras have an additional c-variable that we discuss in more detail in the next
example.
Example 7.6 (Full DAHA of type An). There is a caveat to the discussion above. Cherednik’s full DAHA
Hℏ,c(Cn, Sn) of An-type contains an additional variable c. As explained in [Sim17, Section 2.4.3], the full
DAHA does not arise from the orbifold perspective alone, since the full DAHA is not a deformation of the
group algebra of the braid group. Instead, it is a deformation of its central extension.
Two groups, [JV21] and [MS21b], realized that, in order to remedy this, one needs to work with the
braid group of the once-punctured torus T 2 \ {pt} instead. In [MS21b], the additional relation involving
the c-variable is introduced for isotopies “passing through” the puncture. It is shown [MS21b, Theorem
3.5] (see also [JV21, Proposition 4.5]) that this algebra is isomorphic to the full DAHA. We note that this
relation can be implemented at the level of Sn-paths in (T 2)n by adding an additional relation involving c
for “passing through” the Sn-orbit of the hypersurface pt ×(T 2)n−1.
In [HTY26], the authors showed that the cylindrical version of the A∞-algebra of endomorphisms
of T ∗
[x] Symn T 2 does recover DAHA. In the cylindrical interpretation, instead of counting orbicurves in

which extends our isomorphism from Example 7.1. This is a restatement of [HTY26, Theorem 1.4] for T 2.
Remark 7.7 (Braid surface Hecke algebras). In [HTY26] the braid surface Hecke algebras Hℏ,c([Σn/Sn])
were introduced for an arbitrary closed Riemann surface Σ, extending the deﬁnition of [MS21b]. It is
claimed that the isomorphism
HW ∗

holds for any closed Riemann surface of genus ≥1. We point out that in their proof (see [HTY26, Section
6]) it is tacitly assumed that Hℏ,c([Σn/Sn]) is ﬂat, which is then used to obtain the said isomorphism as
in Lemma 6.15. However, this ﬂatness is not known for genus ≥2, and does not seem to follow directly
from Theorem 6.4. It would be interesting to see whether methods of [Eti17, Section 3] allow one to prove
ﬂatness of the full Hℏ,c([Σn/Sn]), and not only of Hℏ([Σn/Sn]).
In [HKTY25, Theorem 1.7], it is shown that the A∞-algebra CW ∗

to a certain explicit differential graded algebra Hn, deﬁned in terms of generators and relations. From this

holds, and, moreover, the differential in Hn witnesses non-ﬂatness of the Hecke algebra Hℏ(Symn S2).
Example 7.8 (Ball quotients). Consider the n-dimensional complex hyperbolic space H = Hn
C and a
ﬁnitely-generated discrete subgroup Γ ⊂PSU(n, 1) acting on it. By Selberg’s lemma, there exists a ﬁnite
index subgroup Γ′ ⊂Γ acting freely on H. Then for X = H/Γ′ and G = Γ/Γ′ the Hecke algebra is ﬂat,
and Theorem 6.6 applies if Γ is cocompact.
For n = 2 there is a family of Mostow groups Γ(p, t) ⊂PU(2, 1) for p ∈Z>0 and t ∈Q (some of
which were ﬁrst introduced in [Mos80]), which are generated by three complex reﬂections R1, R2, R3 with
rotation parameter given by ζp, and related via conjugation, i.e., there is a matrix J such that R2 = JR1J−1
and R3 = J−1R1J, and t ∈Q is specialized by requiring
Tr(R1J) = e

For a more detailed description, we refer to [DPP21, Section 3]. We also note that these groups are known to
be isomorphic to Deligne-Mostow groups, which arise as monodromy groups of hypergeometric functions
[DM86].
Only 39 pairs (p, t) give rise to a lattice, and such lattices are called Deligne-Mostow lattices. We refer
to [Pas16, Section 3] and [DPP21, Appendix A.9] for a full list of these lattices. As Deraux shows [Der24,
Proposition 3.4], Γ(p, t) is cocompact if and only if the group does not contain parabolic elements. Using
the table [Pas16, Table 1] or the table in [DPP21, Appendix 9] one sees that only the following 33 groups

Example 7.9 (Global quotients of projective spaces). Given a complex representation V of a ﬁnite group
G, one associates the orbifold Hecke algebra to the pair (X = P(V ), G). It was studied in [BM14], and
[BM14, Lemma 5.4.1] provides a way to compute it in terms of Hℏ(V, G). Theorem 6.6 does not apply
here, and we expect the A∞-algebra of the cotangent ﬁber in this case to give rise to an interesting derived
orbifold Hecke algebra.
7.2. Noncompact examples. Before we list the examples of interest, we state the following:
Conjecture 7.10. The isomorphism of Theorem 6.6 also holds in the case of a noncompact complex mani-
fold X equipped with the action of the ﬁnite subgroup G ⊂Aut(X).
In the case of the trivial group G, the above statement follows from [GPS24, Corollary 6.1]. However,
their proof is not a generalization of the theorems of [AS10a] and [Abo12], and is rather a corollary of their
main result [GPS24, Theorem 1.1], which gives an isomorphism between the wrapped Fukaya category of
T ∗X and the category Shc(X) of compact objects in the dg category of sheaves of dg Z-modules on X.
Currently, it is not known whether such an isomorphism extends to the orbifold case.
Remark 7.11. We comment on the approach to proving Conjecture 7.10. The main nontrivial step is the
extension of the Abbondandolo-Schwarz map [AS10a] to the case of manifolds with boundary (which one
may further extend to the noncompact case by exhaustion), and it is not yet present in the literature: it
would require carefully combining their methods with the choices of Floer data used in [GPS20]. After
this is done, one also needs to extend Abouzaid’s evaluation map from [Abo12, Section 5] to the case of
manifolds with boundary for which the techniques of [GPS20, Section 2] should be sufﬁcient. Showing
that the extension of Abouzaid’s evaluation map is the inverse of the extended Abbondandolo-Schwarz map
should be straightforward. We note that using linear Hamiltonians as in [Abo15] could help overcome
some difﬁculties on this path.
Once the above is carried out, the proof of the conjecture would follow the steps of the proof of Theo-
rem 6.6.
Remark 7.12. In [HTY26, Lemma 7.5], the authors claim that the Abbondandolo-Schwarz map extends to
the case of cotangent bundles of punctured surfaces. However, the proof of this is missing in [HTY26] and
should be ﬁxed, potentially using the approach from Remark 7.11.
Example 7.13 (Hecke algebras of complex reﬂection groups). Let G be an irreducible complex reﬂection
group, and X = V be its reﬂection representation. Then Hℏ([X/G]) is the Hecke algebra of [BMR98].
This example also includes the classical Hecke algebras of the Weil groups of simple Lie groups.
In this particular case, by taking X to be a unit ball near the origin, one can in fact prove Conjecture 7.10,
as the required isomorphism for the restriction to ℏ= 0 is straightforward, since there are no Reeb chords
for the standard metric. Hence, the strategy of the proof of Theorem 6.6 extends to this case, once one picks
Floer data that guarantees compactness as in [GPS20] (in a different setting, a similar compactness result is
shown in [HTY26, Lemma 7.4]).
Example 7.14 (Afﬁne Hecke algebras). Let G be a simple Lie group, W be its Weyl group, and V be the
reﬂection representation of W. Let Q∨be the dual root lattice of W. Then
X = V/Q∨
is a complex algebraic torus. The algebra Hℏ([X/W]) is the afﬁne Hecke algebra Hℏ(V, W).

73
Example 7.15 (Hecke algebras of punctured surfaces and their symmetric powers). In Example 7.4, if we
drop the condition for Γ ⊂Aut(H) to act cocompactly, one obtains a punctured orbifold surface [H/Γ].
The Hecke algebras from Example 7.4 extend to this setting.
In the case of Γ acting on H freely, the Hecke algebra Hℏ([Σn/Sn]) was considered in [HTY26, Section
7], where Σ = H/Γ.

by F (we use the holomorphic structure on E induced from the trivialization). There is also a sheaf
O(Eorb, F orb) on D of holomorphic sections of Eorb with boundary condition given by F orb (notice
that along ∂Σ the action of µm is free, hence it is clear how to descend F to F orb), where locally near the
stacky points such holomorphic sections are deﬁned as in [CR04, Section 4] via equivariant sections in the
local orbifold charts. We note that in our global setup, we have
O(Eorb, F orb) ∼= (π∗O(E, F))µm,
(A.8)


## Page 75


SMOOTHING ORBICURVES AND HECKE ALGEBRAS
75
which follows from the deﬁnition. The main observation of [CR04, Proposition 4.2.2] in our case with
boundary is the isomorphism of sheaves
O(Eorb, F orb) ∼= O(|Eorb|, F orb).
(A.9)
The ﬁrst claim of the lemma follows since these are just isomorphisms of cohomologies of sheaves (π∗O(E, F))µm
and O(|Eorb|, F orb).
Now, the pullback arises as follows: over a small neighborhood U ⊂D away from the stacky points, we
have compatible trivializations |Eorb|U ∼= U × Cn and E ∼= π−1(U) × Cn, hence on such U the pullback
is deﬁned as usual.
On a chart U ′ ∼= D2 near a ramiﬁcation p where E trivializes as E|U′ ∼= D2 × V with µm(p) acting via
standard rotations on D2, and V = Resµm
µm(p) V is the restricted representation to the stabilizer µm(p) of p.
We pick a basis V ∼= Cn with action
ζm(p)(w1, . . . , wn) = (ζj1
m(p)w1, . . . , ζjn
m(p)wn).
Recall that |E|orb|π(U′) is trivialized via extension to π(p) of the trivialization
ΨU′ : D2 \ {0} × Cn →D2 \ {0} × Cn
(z, w1, . . . , wn) 7→(zm, z−j1w1, . . . , z−jnwn)
(A.10)
as deﬁned in [CR04, Section 4.2]. The main observation here is that a smooth section of |E|orb|π(U′) pulls
back to a smooth µm(p)-equivariant section of E|U. We notice, however, that the converse is not true: take
n = 1 and j1 = 1 and section ξ(z) = z2¯z, which is equivariant with pushforward ξ(x) = |x|2/m in
coordinate x = zm.
Hence, the following commutative diagram for the standard ﬁne resolutions of sheaves (π∗O(E, F))µm
and O(|Eorb|, F orb) holds; see [KL06, Section 3.4]

∼

,
where each of the maps is naturally induced from the pullback map Ψ∗. Since Ψ∗induces the isomorphisms
between sheaves of holomorphic sections, it induces the isomorphisms on cohomology, hence the result
follows.
